#7537. Why more brands are paying creators to make their ads

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of an ongoing series detailing the most important information readers need to know about the ad industry’s most complex topics. More from the series →

Brands paying influencers to make campaign creative have become key drivers of investment in the booming creator economy. Creative agencies might want to take note.

“You get the authentic, scroll-stopping content that people want to engage each with. But you also get the reach,” said Katy Ball, head of social at M&C Saatchi Sport & Entertainment.

As spending on paid social channels — and video in particular — continues to rise, demand for creative assets to appear in those ad slots is increasing.

The number of assets attached to individual campaigns has risen 30% in the last five years, according to a CreativeX study of 115,990 ad campaigns, representing $11 billion in ad spend. Per the study, just five assets receive 88% of media investment in an average campaign.

The need for more campaign assets is fueling the growth of asset-led spending, the fastest growing segment of the creator economy, according to a report published this week by research consultancy Madison & Wall.

Currently, 12% of the $112 billion spent on creators globally goes towards creators producing ads on behalf of brands, per the study. That’s expected to rise 16% every year through 2030.

The days when brands principally paid creators to rent their following are rapidly disappearing. Influencers are now media partners, ambassadors and increasingly, cogs in the creative production supply chain. And brands see creator-made ads as a solution to their social asset shortage.

“For us, the appeal is simple. Buying space in someone’s feed doesn’t mean you’ve earned their attention. Good creators understand what their audiences care about and how to give them something worth stopping for,” said Jared Kozel, co-founder and chief creative officer at agency Super Nice. “That’s valuable creative judgment to bring into an ad no matter the brand or category.”

Influencer marketing experts note that several different but related practices are included under the broader umbrella of creator-led production.

While some brands simply pay creators a fee in order to receive video or still assets for use in a campaign, whitelisting (also called “dark advertising”) has become a favored method of managing such arrangements.

In this context, an influencer grants a brand limited access to their social media accounts, allowing them to book a paid media campaign through that account, using assets made by the creator while taking advantage of discrete audience targeting.

“What we’re seeing is a lot of recutting of media spend to be allocated towards creator development in order to fund whitelisting,” said Olivia Wedderburn, head of influence at Born Social.

Madison & Wall’s research suggested that funding for creative production may also be coming from PR, social or experimental budgets, as well as traditional media or production pots of money.

Another related practice, user generated content, sees brands paying regular social media users to license their content as creative assets in ad campaigns.

“They have to feed their their content engines, and they’re opening up as many different avenues as they can,” said Andy Ryan, co-founder at social and influencer agency Komodo.

The relative low cost of working with influencers, versus creative production agencies, is the primary driver behind brand spending on creator-made assets.

Although production spending is often a small fraction of an overall campaign budget, marketers are keen to squeeze every non-working (that is, non-media) dollar for all it’s worth. While that same desire is pushing some brands toward generative AI production techniques, others are turning to creators.

But as this type of spending grows, so to does the need to manage it effectively. “It’s never been more complicated to negotiate usage rights or exclusivity,” said Wedderburn.

Billion Dollar Boy, for example, recently added features to its Companion creator management tool designed to better facilitate creator-led production efforts and manage usage rights negotiations.

“The creator economy has never been more competitive, heightening the urgency for brands to understand the market context,” said Thomas Walters, the agency’s chief innocation officer and co-founder.

At the same time, increased brand spending in this area is already leading to an uptick in the fees commanded by some creators. Last year, agency practitioners reported creator rates ticking up 10-20% due to demand for expanded rights.

There’s also some evidence to show that creator-made assets perform as well as, or even better than, traditional advertising spots in a social context.

A report by System1, WPP Media and Goat published in June found that creator ads achieved 3.2X the brand-awareness lift for category leader ads as traditional advertising creative.

“We’re seeing a lot of brands say that is more valuable in terms of conversion and reach than some of the quick-and-dirty assets they previously would have relied on,” said Wedderburn.

That’s not unexpected. Audiences recognize the visual cues, language and production methods employed by creators as native to TikTok or Instagram, while clocking films shot on a closed set or using more traditional methods as foreign that same environment, a lesson that major advertisers like Nike are reluctantly absorbing.

“If programs are set up correctly, creators can deliver volume and variety faster and more cost effectively than a traditional production cycle,” said Nicholas Spiro, chief commercial officer, Viral Nation. “The shift we’re seeing is brands starting to plan creators across their media budgets, rather than as a separate influencer line item alone.”

Consulting giants Deloitte Digital and Accenture Song have started selling marketing in the C-suite with a fresh push.

With shareholders, an $895 billion compute bill and a costly free tier, Anthropic’s no-ads pledge may have a shelf life.

Why the Daily Mail’s ad sales team is turning talent agent.

#7533. Anthropic says no to ads but Wall Street may have other ideas

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Anthropic is a public company now, which puts it on an unofficial path to an ads business.

Unofficial because the company’s official position is still firmly against ads. It went as far as spending Super Bowl money on a campaign mocking OpenAI for bringing them to ChatGPT. Its prospectus, the document pitching those public shares to investors, leaves advertisers out of the business altogether.

“Is it cynical to expect Anthropic to follow the same route? Not really. More like an educated guess,” said ad tech expert Shirley Marschall.

Besides, the company has already left the door open, she continued. Its pledge rules out sponsored links adjacent to conversations, but in the same post it says it will keep building features that help users “find, compare, or buy products, connect with businesses.” And once an agent recommends products, retail media will pay to be in those recommendations, Marschall said, before adding: “Let’s see Anthropic turn that ad revenue down,” she added.

Advertisers, for their part, will be watching closely, and with mixed feelings. On the one hand, a new media seller means a new platform to wrangle and one more set of tools and teams to learn at a time when most are still working out how to buy ChatGPT. On the other hand, Claude’s audience is also spending more and more time in those conversation threads. For marketers, that’s a hard place to stay out of for long.

“Anthropic, like any company, has a stance towards handling consumer data and inadvertently how advertising is considered,” said independent ad tech consultant Jonathan D’Souza Rauto. “But monetization is a lucrative form of revenue. I’d expect Anthropic to have a change of heart. Look no further to how Apple has played this advertising game over the past few years despite being considered anti-advertising.”

Life as a public company isn’t cheap, after all, and it’s even less cheap for an AI company that has signed up for approximately $895.4 billion in compute and infrastructure commitments, some running to 2045. That’s more than 13 years of revenue at its current pace, given Q3 revenue of $17 billion. The revenue covering that bill is far less locked in since 80% of revenue is consumption-based and the S-1 says many customers can cut usage or terminate on short notice. In fact, the company has already warned that “operating margins may compress when large tranches arrive.”

Then there’s the free tier. “Free and promotional users may never convert to paid plans,” the filing admits, yet Anthropic still pays to serve every one of them. Analysts found that cost sits in sales and marketing out of sight of the headline margin. That arrangement holds as long as free users keep upgrading. If they stop, Anthropic is left with a growing audience it pays to serve and earns nothing from.

OpenAI has already shown what that audience could be worth. ChatGPT ads reached a $1 billion annualized revenue run rate less than 200 days after launching. When both companies are trading, analysts will ask Anthropic why it’s leaving money on the table.

“They’ll go after advertising, just not in the way some assume,” said Rob Webster, founder and CEO of TAU Marketing Solutions. “The prize for Anthropic isn’t ads inside Claude. Advertising is now a trillion-dollar market, and the real power sits with whatever brain coordinates it: deciding what runs, where, and why. That’s a far more valuable position than selling impressions. Anthropic wants to be a new, better Microsoft or Apple. Selling tools to businesses. Not ad opportunities.”

There are reasons, though, why Anthropic may resist the lure of ads for as long as it feasibly can.

“Trust and impartiality really matter with LLMs, particularly among enterprise customers,” said Alex Tait, founder of Entropy. “In contrast to OpenAI, Anthropic is currently approaching operating break-even without ads suggesting its existing business model could be commercially sustainable without it.”

Anthropic’s anti-ads stance is based on its perspective that once ads become part of a business’s revenue targets, the pressure to maximize engagement can start influencing product decisions. For an AI app, in this case Claude, that creates a conflict: users expect Claude to help them make decisions in their own interests, not towards whatever generates revenue for the company or its advertisers. And in its IPO prospectus, the company said that they want “Claude’s only incentive to be giving them [users] a helpful answer,” which is why they have “chosen not to introduce that pressure [ads] into Claude.”

Anthropic did not respond to Digiday’s request for comment.

Consulting giants Deloitte Digital and Accenture Song have started selling marketing in the C-suite with a fresh push.

#7531. Future of Marketing Briefing: Consulting firms revive their marketing ambitions in the C-suite the holdcos covet

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of Marketing Briefing covers the latest in marketing for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Friday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

However the holdcos settle the question of whether the CEO route counts as due diligence, Deloitte Digital and Accenture Song got there first, and both have started selling marketing in the C-suite with a fresh push.

Deloitte Digital’s offer is Converge, an operating system that strings together AI agents to run marketing and sales work, from research and briefs through to campaign deployment and deal tracking. Some of the agents are Deloitte’s own. Others are ones a client already runs elsewhere, and the whole thing plugs into the tools and clout contracts a brand is already paying for. Marketing and sales versions are live, with commerce on the way, and Deloitte pitches them as one front-office system tied to revenue, which it calls “brand to cash.” Because it’s sold as a license that stretches across sales, marketing and service, it needs sign-off well above the CMO, which suits a consulting firm that’s used to having those conversations.

Accenture Song’s play in media, a market it has circled in the U.S. for years without committing. In September, it moved Melissa Fein, who built its media planning and buying business in Australia and New Zealand after leaving Initiative in 2024, to New York as U.S. media lead. This month it posted a job for a head of media strategy for the Americas. The ad describes a “new world media practice” with no legacy systems to protect, and asks for an AI-first strategist” to lead planning, build new tools and become the person senior clients call before a brief exists.

Candidates need hands-on command of econometrics, incrementality testing and attribution plus experience in big-spending categories like financial services, telecommunications, automotive and health. Those are the categories where the ANZ team won Australia Post and Mediabank. Accenture Song Ndidi Oteh has kept quiet on specifics, saying only that whatever it does in media won’t take traditional forms.

“The real prize is the conversation before the brief,” said  Steve Boehler, founder of agency consultancy the Mercer Island Group. ”If you help define the business problem, design the operating model and decide how success will be measured, you are competing long before an agency review begins.”

Both plays echo what the holdcos have spent 18 months engineering: operating systems that run a client’s marketing for it and media businesses that sell more than buying. The consultants may have the better shot at making either one stick. They already sit with the CEOs, CIOs and CFOs who sign off on systems spanning sales, marketing and service and installing that kind of software is their day job. A holdco operating system, rightly or wrongly, still reads as an agency tool, built partly to make the agency cheaper to run. Accenture also arrives in the U.S. media with no legacy buying business to protect, so it can lead with measurement and tools before it has to sell buying power.

Whether any of this translates is another matter. Consultancies have been trying to crack advertising for the best part of a decade, after all, and the media buying business still rewards a scale Accenture doesn’t yet have in the U.S. Then again, now is arguably as good a time as any for them to try, if not better. With Omnicom and IPG combined, big advertisers are down to three holdcos, and some are skipping the pitch one so a credible alternative has rarely been more useful. AI is also pushing decisions about how marketing works into rooms where the CFO and CIO sit, which consultancies have been in for years. And a C-suite that takes a closer look at marketing will start asking how agencies make their money, from data fees to principal media, which is an awkward question for any firm with a buying business to defend.

“The consultancies may have an advantage in getting invited to design the system,” said Boehler. “That is not the same as proving they can run marketing better. The client still needs evidence of better decisions, better work and better business outcomes, not just fewer handoffs.”

All of which makes the Publicis-WPP episode look a little quaint, partly because it was never quite the fight it was billed as. Sadoun spent months telling investors that Publicis was winning big accounts without a pitch. Rose, asked about it by an analyst, called it “this pitchless CEO handshake thing that I keep getting asked about” and said decisions that big deserve careful consideration. Publicis answered with an open letter treating that line as her theory, and the coverage did the rest. Somewhere along the way, a question about how clients choose agencies turned into a row about manners.

What got lost was the shift both CEOs were describing. Big advertisers are increasingly buying data, identity and AI infrastructure, which a six-week parade of slide decks was never built to judge, so some now test one partner in depth instead of auditioning several. Those decisions are also moving up the organization chart, to executives who sign off on systems not campaigns. That’s how consultancies have always been bought. Deloitte and Accenture are built for long, technical evaluations run by CFOs and CIOs, and while the holdcos; remarks were being turned into a grudge march one launched a system that needs the c-suite’s signature and the other started building a U.S. media practice designed to win it.

“Client CEOs and CMOs pay attention to CEOs of holdcos, especially when a transition in agency leadership occurs and the agency CEO conducts outreach to key clients,” said Gartner analyst Jay Wilson. “But most recognize that while agency CEOs may set a direction for the agency, that direction may be slow to take hold – or never take hold – within the agency teams doing the actual day-to- day work.  A strong relationship with, or aversion to, an agency chief executive is usually more consequential at smaller, independent agencies.”

Whatever gets agreed in the c-suite, someone still has to do the work.

“The consultants don’t have to copy the incumbent model to compete,” said Boehler. “But they still have to do the work: plan, negotiate, deliver, reconcile and prove results. A different model is a proposition until clients experience the difference.”

Cinema admissions have climbed back on the strength of a fuller blockbuster slate and much of the ad money that shifted to digital during lockdowns stayed put. Most screens still sell their preshow through sales houses that have always sold offline.

Mikkel Hagedorn, a WPP and Omnicom alum, has spent five or six years building a platform that lets buyers book cinema spots programmatically, target them against specific films and count impressions straight off ticket sales. The company is live in Denmark and Finland, where sales houses have handed it two minutes of a 15-minute preshow at a base CPM of around €75. Hagedorn’s bet is that exhibitors can bring cinema into the digital ad market while keeping the premium pricing newspapers gave away when they went programmatic.

His company, called Cinemataztic, gives buyers two ways to purchase inventory: first, agencies already running programmatic campaigns can reach the inventory through supply-side platforms and demand-side platforms such as Smartclip Google’s DV360 respectively, which lets cinema sit inside budgets that were going to be spread across other channels anyway, Advertisers who would rather skip the intermediary fees can book directly through Hagedorn’s own interface.

Either way, the buyer can pick what individual film they want to target, so a brand could run only against the upcoming Avengers: Doomsday movie if it likes. The impressions they buy come straight from exhibitors’ ticketing systems, which means one ticket sold counts as one impression. There are “no multipliers here,” said Hagedorn, a dig at the audience multipliers that inflate numbers elsewhere in media.

There’s also a performance bent to the inventory. Audiences log into a pre-show game, which feeds real-time targeting data and lets advertisers push vouchers to their phones. Hagedorn pointed to a Specsavers campaign as an example. On lead generation campaigns, he claimed an average click-through rate of 10%, a figure display buyers stopped dreaming about years ago.

So far a lot of the money has come from digital agencies edging up the funnel into brand work. Hagedorn said he has seen little cannibalisation of advertisers still buying cinema the old way. He said the business has grown 50% a year and forecasts the same for next year, when the company expands into Sweden, Thailand and a third, bigger market he wouldn’t share.

Even so, the programmatication of cinema will be a slow burn. Advertising is still its third-largest revenue line after tickets and concessions. Most hand it entirely to sales houses on contracts of three to five years and only learn that demand is falling when the next guarantee shrinks. Hagedorn has been warning them about this for years. “They cannot stay offline forever,” he said.

Brands that gatecrash cultural moments tend to stick to tournaments and royal weddings, and they rarely pick a game that lets players mug strangers in a fake Florida. Grand Theft Auto, the culture-warping franchise, is the exception, and its sixth installment is drawing the kind of guerilla marketing normally reserved for FIFA and the Olympics.

Anyone trying to keep up can follow the running tally at GTA6Brands.com, an independent tracker run by Mike Litman that logs a new stunt each day for his GTA 6 Marketing News podcast. Hardly anything in culture still sets off that kind of scramble across so many brands at once, and with GTA the explanation is scale.

“I’ve tracked over 290 brand moves around GTA 6 so far, including around 130 unofficial activations,” said Litman. “That’s a remarkable amount of marketing activity orbiting a product launch, particularly when so many of those brands have no formal relationship with Rockstar. What’s interesting is the range of brands getting involved.”

With media this fragmented, marketers have a shortlist of moments when a mass audience shows up at the same time, and most of those moments involve a ball. GTA earned its spot on that list over 13 years. When GTA 5 launched in 2013 it made $1 billion in three days, which owner Take Two called a rate faster than any other video game, film or other entertainment product has ever managed. For context, Marvel’s “The Avengers” movie took 19 days to reach that number. In the years since it launched, the game has since surpassed 230 million units sold worldwide across three console generations. The wait for the sequel turned into an event of its own. GTA 6’s second trailer received 475 million views across all platforms within 24 hours of its release, beating the 365 million in 24 hours for Marvel’s Deadpool & Wolverine film. Take Two has issued $8 billion for its 2027 guidance for the fiscal year the game launches in. For a brand that wants a crowd all watching the same thing at once, that’s about as close to a World Cup final as entertainment gets, and nobody has to get past FIFA’s lawyers.

“I think the fascinating thing is that GTA 6 has achieved something usually reserved for the World Cup or Super Bowl, but without the stadium, the broadcast rights or the traditional sponsorship machinery,” said Litman. “It’s become a cultural event in its own right. And brands aren’t waiting for permission to participate.”

A few did get permission, though. Rockstar’s list of official partners is short and made up mostly of platforms. Sony signed an exclusive marketing deal, which put a “Plays Best on PS5” tag on the game’s official channels. Netflix got the “Extended Look” trailer six trailers before YouTube in August, in what Take Two boss Strauss Zelnick called a “groundbreaking partnership”. YouTube is running a 24-hour launch live livestream from its own channel, and the Miami Heat have redesigned court, custom uniforms and Vice City merchandise.

“Nike is going back to its roots and what built the brand: cultural relevance through people who have the trust of a community. It is a bold choice when most advertising pitches are about AI. Nike uses AI too, as a tool, but clearly shows it understands that what stays scarce is trust. Trust comes from identity and proximity: who is speaking, and how close they are to the community listening. It also shows what creators and athletes must build to ask a brand like Nike for more than just a campaign fee. Most of them are offering access to an audience that belongs to a social or tech platform, targeted and measured with the data that the platform makes available. Global stars aside, only those who directly own the relationship with their audience and their data can negotiate a share of the long-term value.”

— Alessandro De Zanche, founder of media consultancy ADZ Strategies on Nike’s bet on creators to drive growth.

#7528. Attorney, New Verticals at Atticus

At any given time, 16 million Americans are experiencing a crisis that requires urgent help from our legal system or government. The right assistance could transform their lives. But today, most never get it.

Atticus makes it easy for any sick or injured person in crisis to get the life-changing aid they deserve. In the last six years, we've become the leading platform connecting people with disabilities to government benefits. We’re also quickly scaling an injury practice to help victims of accidents, misconduct, and violence get compensation from insurance.

So far, we've helped hundreds of thousands of people access over $10B in life-changing aid — and earned over 20,000+ five-star reviews. And we're just getting started.

We've raised more than $100 million from top VC firms like Forerunner, GV (Google Ventures), and True Ventures, with ambitions to create a category defining business assisting Americans in need.

Each month, tens of thousands of Americans seek our help for a single legal matter – applying for Social Security Disability, filing a Workers’ Compensation claim – but many of these clients are actually eligible for additional aid without ever knowing it.

Your job as Lead Attorney, New Verticals is to help us do right by these clients, helping them claim additional benefits through the social safety net. You’ll oversee the team of intake specialists who speak with clients who have secondary claims, evaluating the case facts and whether to connect them with an Atticus attorney or local attorney to provide legal advice and potentially take on their case.

This is a job that straddles the line between practicing the law and growing a business. It requires developing a strong understanding of multiple fields of law, and being able to accurately identify when we can help and when we cannot. But even more importantly, this job requires you to be an incredibly strong operator: you need to keep the big picture in mind and stay laser-focused on how we can achieve our goals, oftentimes finding outside-the-box ways to accomplish them. You will report to our General Manager of New Verticals and work closely with our CEO and Managing Attorney.

On any given day, you’ll spend your time:

Speaking with severely injured clients trying to rebuild their lives after a catastrophic accident

Sourcing and vetting local counsel in individual markets, holding a high bar for representation quality and ending relationships that fall below it

Working with our data and insights team to identify existing clients who may have unrecognized claims in adjacent fields of law, and setting the standard for which ancillary cases we take on

Building the playbook for claim types we haven't handled before, starting by screening cases yourself and eventually creating the protocol our intake specialists will run at scale

This is a rare opportunity for an attorney to join a profitable and growing public-interest law firm and take on one of our biggest priorities as a company.

You can understand the nuances of various contingency fields of law and can apply them to a variety of cases (including Social Security Disability, Workers’ Compensation, Personal Injury, and more) – you don’t need to be an expert today, but you should be a fast learner.

You can empathize with anyone and quickly build rapport and relationships while exercising good judgment, both with our clients and local counsel at law firms across the country.

You have strong instincts and judgment as an operator – you can run effective and efficient processes, and you don’t let them run you.

You have a demonstrated commitment to doing good and helping people.

You’ve built a track record as a great people manager – you know how to motivate talent and hold a high bar for output.

You have meaningful work experience outside of serving as a lawyer, ideally at a startup or other fast-paced, agile environment (e.g., a business operations team).

We are strongly committed to building a diverse team. If you're from a background that's underrepresented in tech, we'd love to meet you.

This role offers a unique chance to join a startup that has strong traction (substantial funding, well-respected backers, tremendous growth, and many happy customers) but is still small enough that you can have a huge impact and play a role in shaping our success.

We're a certified B Corporation tackling a critical social problem. Our mission to help people in need drives everything we do, and your work here will touch many lives.

We offer competitive pay — including equity — and generous benefits:

Medical and dental insurance with 100% of employees and their families premiums covered

15 vacation days & ~20 paid holidays each year (including two weeks at end-of-year)

$1,000 reimbursable stipend for education and training outside of work

Up to $1,200/year student loan repayment assistance

Humble, thoughtful, smart, fun colleagues

We anticipate the base salary band for this role will be between $140,000 and $175,000 in addition to equity and benefits. The salary at offer will be determined by a number of factors such as candidate's experience, knowledge, skills and abilities, as well as internal equity among our team.

This job is fully remote and we’re committed to empowering everyone with flexibility. Work remotely, and travel to LA (on the company dime) as needed to be with your colleagues – usually quarterly, plus offsites. We care a lot about building a great culture and we think some interactions need to happen in person, so we put a lot of thought into retreats, offsites, and other ways to gather.

#7527. Week of Nov 9 Cable News Ratings: Fox News and CNN Share the Spoils

This is TVNewser’s basic cable network ranker and cable news report for the week of November 9, 2026.

As expected, the three cable networks could not maintain their midterm election-week momentum, declining in total viewers and the Adults 25-54 demo during primetime and total day.

Viewer interest remained high during the just-concluded week, driven by the breaking news of the FAA issuing a nationwide airplane groundstop on Friday, Nov. 13. Fox News was the most-watched network in total viewers on that day, while CNN finished on top in the prized demo.

Interestingly, the FAA breaking news kept CNN in first place in the primetime demo for the second week in a row.

According to Nielsen Big Data + Panel, for the week of Nov. 9, Fox News averaged 1.584 million total viewers and 109,000 A25-54 viewers during primetime. The network saw losses of 15% in total viewers and 33% in the demo compared with the prior week (the week beginning Nov. 2).

During total day viewing, Fox News averaged 1.018 million total viewers and 93,000 A25-54 viewers. Based on the previous week’s average, the network was down by 9% in total viewers and 25% in the demo.

Fox News remained second in total primetime viewership and slipped from third to fourth in demo viewership among all basic cable networks. During total day, it remained first in total viewers and was in third place in the total day demo.

MS NOW averaged 1.077 million total viewers and 109,000 viewers in the A25-54 demo for the week that just concluded. The network dropped by 30% in total viewers and 52% in the demo compared with its prior-week primetime average.

During total day, MS NOW averaged 674,000 total viewers and 73,000 demo viewers. It was down 27% in total viewers and 42% in the demo compared with the network’s average the prior week.

Among all basic cable networks, MS NOW continued to be in third in total primetime viewers and fell from fourth to ninth in the demo. It ranked third in total viewers and was sixth in the total day demo.

CNN averaged 802,000 total primetime viewers and 160,000 demo viewers. It saw declines of 40% in total viewers and 57% in the demo compared to the previous week.

During total day, the network had 530,000 total viewers and 85,000 demo viewers. For CNN, total viewers were down 29%, and demo viewers were down 49% during this daypart.

Among all cable networks, CNN remained in fourth place in total viewers and was the No. 3 network in the demo during primetime. It continued to be in fourth in total day among total viewers and was in the same position in the demo.

Compared to their performance during the same period a year ago:

Fox News held 9 of the 15 spots among the week’s most-watched cable news shows, with The Five in first place with 2.562 million total viewers at 5 p.m. ET. MS NOW’s top show was the 9 p.m. ET Monday airing of The Rachel Maddow Show in fourth place with 1.904 million total viewers. MS NOW had a total of six programs in the top 15 for the just-concluded week.

The 15 top spots in the Adults 25-54 demo were populated with shows from all three cable networks. CNN’s Anderson Cooper 360, airing weeknights at 8 p.m. ET, was in first place with 229,000 demo viewers. CNN had a total of five shows in the top 15. Fox News’ Jesse Watters Primetime, airing weeknights at 8 p.m. ET, finished second with 223,000 demo viewers. Fox News had seven shows on the chart. Finally, MS NOW’s The Rachel Maddow Show was the network’s top show, with 166,000 demo viewers, ranking 12th. MS NOW had four shows in the chart.

#7524. Engineering Manager - Pipelines at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're looking for an Engineering Manager to lead the Pipelines team at Supabase. Supabase Pipelines is a managed change data capture product. It copies the tables you select from Supabase Postgres to other systems, then streams later changes. The product is in public alpha, with BigQuery, ClickHouse, DuckLake and Snowflake destinations.

Pipelines uses Postgres logical replication and Supabase ETL, our open source replication engine written in Rust. The team works close to the database. That means replication slots, WAL retention, initial table syncs, and the load they place on a customer's source database. The team also builds and runs the destination connectors. Correctness, ordering and recovery matter there as much as speed.

The Engineering Manager is a people, delivery and technical role. We don't expect you to write production code. We do expect you to join design discussions and help set the bar for engineering quality, testing and operations.

Build a healthy, high-trust team culture in a remote-first, globally distributed environment.

Run 1:1s, performance management, career development, and onboarding for your direct reports.

Handle people admin (leave, time off, etc.) for your team.

Own the hiring plan for your team and act as Hiring Manager. First round of interviewer for all roles on the team.

Partner with Talent Acquisition to source, interview, and close strong candidates.

Contribute to headcount planning, org design, and the evolution of engineering leadership at Supabase.

Own the team's roadmap and project management (Linear), driving accountability and clear milestones.

Manage workflow intake and Slack triage. These are the team’s primary inboxes for cross-team requests. You decide what gets routed where, prioritized, or pushed back on.

Own the team's weekly updates and keep stakeholders aligned without creating overhead.

Organize effort against support tickets and maintain the team's SLO.

Surface risks, dependencies, and tradeoffs early and clearly to leadership.

Train as an Incident Commander. Drive coordination, resolution, and post-incident follow-up.

Have 5+ years of engineering background, with enough depth to be credible with engineers building complex systems.

Have 3+ years managing remote engineering teams.

Have managed a team that built and ran data pipelines, change data capture, replication or streaming systems.

Have strong technical credibility in data systems. We look for delivery guarantees, ordering, idempotency, schema changes and backfills.

Know Postgres replication well, including logical replication, the WAL and replication slots, and how they affect a production database.

Know analytical destinations such as BigQuery, Snowflake, ClickHouse or lakehouse table formats like Iceberg.

Have led a team through production incidents on a system that holds customer data. You ran the follow-up work and kept the review blameless.

Have shipped a developer-facing data product to customers outside your company.

Have experience with Rust (not required but a plus)

Have managed remote, globally distributed teams that work asynchronously across disparate time zones.

Are technical enough to be credible with strong engineers, but genuinely passionate about people management. This is the work you want to do, not a track you're settling for.

Build psychological safety and a feedback culture. You give feedback often and in the moment, ask for it back, and create space for the team to do the same.

Operate as a multiplier. You help individuals achieve more than they could alone, and you don't need to be the best technical voice in the room.

Coach rather than direct. You remove blockers, connect people to context, and trust your engineers to own how the work gets done.

Have a real track record of performance management and can speak to specific examples of difficult conversations.

Are comfortable with ambiguity and can build structure where there isn't much.

Communicate clearly and write well. You default to async and written, set context early, and surface risks early.

Are collaborative, low-ego and adaptable. You thrive with high-autonomy senior engineers who don't need you to be the smartest person in the room.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

#7522. Hearts United Wins $110M Pinterest Media AOR in First Pitch Since Launch

Hearts United has won Pinterest’s U.S. and U.K. media account following a competitive pitch, headed by Hwy 111 Media, that included both holding companies and independent agencies, the agency shared with ADWEEK.

#7521. Engineering Manager - Realtime at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're looking for an Engineering Manager to lead the Realtime team at Supabase. Realtime lets developers send low-latency messages between clients, track and synchronize user state, and listen to database changes. Those three features are Broadcast, Presence and Postgres Changes. Broadcast also sends from the database and over HTTP, and clients can replay recent messages.

Realtime is a globally distributed Elixir cluster built on Phoenix. The team balances low latency, fair limits between projects, and the cost of running the service. Customers expect connections to stay up, so reliability matters as much as new features.

The Engineering Manager is a people, delivery and technical role. We don't expect you to write production code, but you you will lead a team of experienced engineers. We do expect you to join design discussions and help set the bar for engineering quality, testing and operations.

Build a healthy, high-trust team culture in a remote-first, globally distributed environment.

Run 1:1s, performance management, career development, and onboarding for your direct reports.

Handle people admin (leave, time off, etc.) for your team.

Own the hiring plan for your team and act as Hiring Manager. First round of interviewer for all roles on the team.

Partner with Talent Acquisition to source, interview, and close strong candidates.

Contribute to headcount planning, org design, and the evolution of engineering leadership at Supabase.

Own the team's roadmap and project management (Linear), driving accountability and clear milestones.

Manage workflow intake and Slack triage. These are the team’s primary inboxes for cross-team requests. You decide what gets routed where, prioritized, or pushed back on.

Own the team's weekly updates and keep stakeholders aligned without creating overhead.

Organize effort against support tickets and maintain the team's SLO.

Surface risks, dependencies, and tradeoffs early and clearly to leadership.

Train as an Incident Commander. Drive coordination, resolution, and post-incident follow-up.

Have 5+ years of engineering background, with enough depth to be credible with engineers building complex systems.

Have 3+ years managing remote engineering teams.

Have managed a team that built and ran a low-latency, high-connection service. Examples are WebSockets, pub/sub, messaging, presence or streaming systems.

Have strong technical credibility in distributed systems. We look for clustering, rate limiting, backpressure and capacity planning.

Have led a team through production incidents on a customer-facing service. You ran the follow-up work and kept the review blameless.

Understand what developers need from a real-time API or SDK, and have shipped one to customers outside your company.

Have experience with Elixir, Erlang or BEAM (not required but a plus).

Have experience with Postgres logical replication and Row Level Security (not required but a plus).

Have managed remote, globally distributed teams that work asynchronously across disparate time zones.

Are technical enough to be credible with strong engineers, but genuinely passionate about people management. This is the work you want to do, not a track you're settling for.

Build psychological safety and a feedback culture. You give feedback often and in the moment, ask for it back, and create space for the team to do the same.

Operate as a multiplier. You help individuals achieve more than they could alone, and you don't need to be the best technical voice in the room.

Coach rather than direct. You remove blockers, connect people to context, and trust your engineers to own how the work gets done.

Have a real track record of performance management and can speak to specific examples of difficult conversations.

Are comfortable with ambiguity and can build structure where there isn't much.

Communicate clearly and write well. You default to async and written, set context early, and surface risks early.

Are collaborative, low-ego and adaptable. You thrive with high-autonomy senior engineers who don't need you to be the smartest person in the room.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

Beyond finding games, the successful candidate will assess their commercial potential, development teams' ability to deliver, and positioning within the competitive landscape.

They will own the scouting pipeline, from identifying opportunities and building developer relationships through to evaluation, internal presentation and negotiation. They will also help establish the processes and standards that will underpin our scouting operation as the business grows.

Focused on impact. You understand the 80/20 rule and focus on activities that matter.

Performance-driven and growth-oriented. You have an intrinsic will to succeed while unafraid to try, fail, and learn by experimenting with new approaches and testing ideas.

Cooperative. You help others succeed; you give and receive feedback well.

Humble. You ask for help; you are quick to admit and learn from your mistakes.

SMART. You work and communicate in ways that are Specific, Measurable, Actionable, Relevant, and Time-bound.

Identify and source exceptional PC and console games through industry networks, events, direct relationships and digital channels. Build a strong pipeline of opportunities, developing relationships with independent developers, studios and industry contacts. Focus initially on Steam and Epic Games Store, identifying distinctive games with genuine commercial potential.

Consider potential for streaming, content creation and community engagement, alongside development team capabilities, budgets, schedules, commercial potential and key risks.

Drive evaluation and signing by reviewing pitches, prototypes and playable builds, producing structured assessments and presenting recommendations internally. Coordinate due diligence, lead or support negotiations with prospective publishing partners, and maintain developer relationships throughout the process.

Build the scouting function by establishing processes, evaluation criteria and pipeline management. Monitor market trends and emerging opportunities, report on potential signings, and help recruit and develop the scouting team as the business grows.

Significant experience in PC/console publishing, scouting, business development or a related commercial role, with an established independent developer and studio network and a track record of identifying and progressing promising games.

Excellent knowledge of PC and console markets, genres, competitors and player expectations, alongside practical Steam experience, including publishing, launching games and building audiences.

Strong creative and commercial judgement, with the ability to evaluate gameplay, market potential, development capability and financial viability.

The confidence to make independent recommendations, challenge assumptions and communicate a compelling commercial rationale, supported by excellent relationship-building, presentation and cross-functional collaboration skills.

Compensation. We pay based on qualifications, experience, and fit for the role. We provide an allowance to purchase your own device or equipment and offer variable compensation through our annual bonus program.

You’ll work closely with Finance, Engineering, Legal, and our vendor partners.

Build the dashboards and reporting hands-on, partner with our data teams to scale them, and own the governance layer (spend policies, thresholds, accountability).

Coordinate the cloud efficiency program: keep optimization initiatives visible and tracked from commitment to realized savings, partnering with Engineering without taking over their delivery.

Have owned vendor relationships, contract negotiations, and renewals, and treat them as a lever on unit economics.

Have run procurement governance such as competitive-bid processes, benchmark analysis, approval thresholds, or managed-procurement partners.

Have high judgment and can hold a position and drive alignment without positional authority.

Build cost dashboards and reporting hands-on (SQL, BI tools), and are comfortable with vendor and cloud spend data (AWS, GCP, SaaS billing) and licensing/usage analysis.

Communicate clearly across both technical and non-technical audiences.

#7508. Legal Assistant at Atticus

At any given time, 16 million Americans are experiencing a crisis that requires urgent help from our legal system or government. The right assistance could transform their lives. But today, most never get it.

Atticus makes it easy for any sick or injured American to get life-changing aid. Our mission is to tear down barriers between people in crisis and the aid they deserve. In the last six years, we’ve become the leading platform connecting people with disabilities to government benefits. We also help victims of accidents, misconduct, and violence get compensation from insurance.

So far, we’ve helped hundreds of thousands of people access over $10 billion in life-changing aid —and earned over 20,000 five-star reviews. And we’re just getting started.

We've raised more than $100 million from top VC firms like Forerunner, Google Ventures, and True Ventures, and we’re on our way to creating a category defining business assisting Americans in need.

Each day, thousands of people seek our help getting disability benefits or insurance compensation. They’re intimidated by the process, unsure where to turn, and desperate for good advice.

As a Legal Assistant at Atticus, your job is to ensure they get and stay on the right path. This job is different from that of a traditional legal assistant. The job mixes responsibilities in any good legal assistant’s comfort zone (build client relationships; support paralegals and attorneys in filing and managing claims) with those unique to being an operator in a fast-growing tech company (help Marketing source clients who’d be great in testimonials, or user test new tools for Product).

In a typical week you might do any of the following:

Make contact with a client that’s in danger of slipping through the cracks at SSA, and help get their case back on track.

Work with a client’s team to run down missing paperwork at SSA.

Take on an inefficient workflow that’s slowing the team down (e.g., processing case updates), scope an improvement, get team buy-in, and execute the improvement.

This role is a rare opportunity to join a fast-growing Series C startup and do meaningful social impact work. It’s designed to be a path to building expertise in the fields of law you support and taking on greater responsibilities over time (e.g. becoming a paralegal).

You have a Bachelor’s degree from a four-year university with strong academic performance.

You have experience in a professional role that required a substantial amount of customer-facing work and/or administrative tasks.

You are a resourceful team-player ready to dive into a scrappy startup environment where you will learn quickly and get your hands dirty.

You are comfortable in a variety of interpersonal conversations; you can be empathetic and kind when speaking with people in a moment of crisis, and can be thoughtful and organized when interacting with attorneys or the SSA.

You have a demonstrated commitment to doing good and helping people.

You have professional experience helping others apply for government benefits.

You have experience at a startup or other fast-paced, agile environment.

We are strongly committed to building a diverse team. If you’re from a background that’s underrepresented in tech, we’d love to meet you.

This is a rare opportunity to join a startup that has strong traction (substantial funding, well-respected backers, tremendous growth, and many happy customers) but is still small enough that you can have a huge impact and play a role in shaping our culture.

We’re a certified B Corporation tackling a critical social problem. Our mission to help people in need drives everything we do, and your work here will touch many lives.

We offer competitive pay — including equity for every employee — and generous benefits:

Medical and dental insurance with 100% of employees and their families premiums covered

15 vacation days & ~20 paid holidays each year (including two weeks at the end-of-year)

$1,000/year reimbursable stipend for education and training outside of work

Up to $1,200/year student loan repayment assistance

Humble, thoughtful, smart, fun colleagues

We anticipate the base salary band for this role will be between $50,000 and $75,000 in addition to equity and benefits. The salary at offer will be determined by a number of factors such as candidate’s experience, knowledge, skills and abilities, as well as internal equity among our team.

This job is fully remote and we’re committed to empowering everyone with flexibility. Work remotely, and travel to LA (on the company dime) as needed to be with your colleagues – usually quarterly, plus offsites. We care a lot about building a great culture and we think some interactions need to happen in person, so we put a lot of thought into retreats, offsites, and other ways to gather.

#7503. Public Policy Lead - US at Kindred

Kindred is a members-only home swapping platform that unlocks the ability to live a travel-rich lifestyle through the power of community. By exchanging primary residences with trusted peers, renters and owners alike can match with Kindred spirits and explore new destinations without breaking the bank.

Kindred is revolutionizing the “third option” of travel accommodation, compared to short-term rentals and hotels. Since launching in 2022, Kindred has grown to a global community of 300,000 members across 150+ cities and puts home swapping on the map as a radically more affordable, responsible, and connected way to travel.

To support that momentum, Kindred has raised over $150M in capital from some of the most credible investors in Silicon Valley, including Index Ventures, Andreessen Horowitz, New Enterprise Associates, Bessemer Venture Partners, Caffeinated Capital, Elad Gil, and founders of companies like Opendoor, Figma, ClassPass, and more.

Kindred’s co-founders are proven leaders from the early team at Opendoor ($15B+ exit), who have each built and scaled products that today generate $1B+ annual revenue combined.

We’re looking for the world’s top builders, executors, and believers to help us make travel more meaningful.

You can learn more about us in Forbes and The New York Times.

This is Kindred’s first dedicated public policy hire. You’ll join a three-person legal and policy team (reporting to the General Counsel) and own the execution of Kindred’s public policy and government relations efforts, with a focus on North American markets but covering additional markets as necessary.

The role is primarily focused on proactive advocacy to shape the policy environment for home-swapping. You’ll build relationships with elected officials, regulators, and industry groups. You’ll commission and manage economic impact research. You’ll develop city-level partnerships that normalize home-swapping as a category distinct from other travel categories. You’ll also develop working familiarity with the tax, housing, and accommodation regulations that intersect with Kindred’s model, and help parse how those frameworks apply (or don’t) to credit-based home-swapping. You’ll do all of this initially as a team of one, with outside consultants to support you and a path to building a team as the company grows and the function matures.

Execute Kindred’s public policy strategy with an emphasis on proactive advocacy.

Build and maintain relationships with elected officials, regulators, and staff at the local, state/provincial, and national levels. Represent Kindred in meetings, hearings, coalition convenings, and industry events.

Track proposed housing, short-term rental, tax, and platform regulations that could impact Kindred’s model, identifying where Kindred’s credit-based, non-commercial structure creates meaningful regulatory distinctions. Use AI-assisted tools and other technology to monitor developments across jurisdictions.

Further develop and evolve Kindred’s core policy narrative and positioning materials. Draft position papers, comment letters, testimony, and advocacy materials.

Propose and negotiate city-level partnerships that promote and normalize home-swapping. Build relationships with housing advocacy organizations, tourism boards, trade associations, and aligned industry coalitions.

Commission and manage third-party economic analyses on the impact of Kindred’s model, collaborating with internal business teams and outside consulting firms.

Identify, evaluate, and manage outside consultants cost-effectively (lobbyists, policy advisors, research firms).

Partner with internal teams to develop externally facing materials and data packages for stakeholder engagements.

Coordinate with the General Counsel on lobbying compliance, political activity disclosures, and regulatory filings.

7–10 years of experience in public policy, with at least 3 years at a technology company or similar fast paced environment.

Experience building or meaningfully contributing to building a public policy or government relations function, not just operating within an established one.

Track record of productive relationships with elected officials, regulators, and policy stakeholders.

Experience managing outside consultants, lobbyists, or policy advisors across multiple jurisdictions.

Strong written and verbal communication skills, including experience drafting policy position papers, advocacy materials, or regulatory comments.

Experience with policy matters at the city and state level in the United States, with an interest in policy work in new jurisdictions.

Fluency with AI tools and a willingness to integrate them into policy monitoring, research, and content development workflows.

Willingness to operate as an individual contributor while building the function, with the judgment to know when to engage outside support.

Experience in or adjacent to the sharing economy, home-sharing, travel, housing, or short-term rental sectors.

Experience at a growth-stage company (under 500 employees).

Familiarity with vacation/short-term rental, housing, and land use regulations, as well as related tax regulations.

Comfort with detailed regulatory analysis: ability to read and interpret statutes, ordinances, and administrative rules, and translate their implications into actionable policy positions. A law degree and legal practice experience is a plus but not required.

Build something new: You’ll help define the function and shape how Kindred engages with governments and regulators from the ground up.

A genuinely different model: Kindred’s credit-based, non-commercial home-swapping model is structurally distinct from existing travel lodging categories, contributing to sustainable tourism objectives.

Path to leadership: As the company and function grow, this role offers the opportunity to greater leadership.

Competitive compensation: Base plus meaningful equity in a growth-stage company.

Audit the sales content reps use today and how product usage currently routes accounts to sales. Set baselines with RevOps for win rate, sales cycle, deal size, and expansion revenue. Share a written point of view with the entire growth team on who our established creator buyer is and the three biggest gaps in how we sell to them. And an action plan on how we can win these creators.

First Year: Sales runs on messaging and tools you built, pitch with confidence and we drive 2X inbound MQLs from when you started. You’ll own and lead our channel strategy, setting the vision, driving cross-functional alignment, and partnering with Marketing to bring it to life. You'll run a quarterly win/loss program and share what you learn openly, build a clear path for sales leads on our website, and make sure launches that matter to larger accounts ship with both a self-serve and a sales-assisted version. Your work has a direct impact on helping Kit reach $100,000,000 ARR while protecting the self-serve engine that got us here.

Segmentation and buyer definition: turning firmographic and product usage data into a clear picture of who to sell to and when

Messaging and positioning for a sales-assisted buyer, including competitive differentiation and objection handling

Sales enablement that reps actually use: demo narratives, discovery guides, business cases, and battlecards

Writing and storytelling that turns product depth into a business case a non-technical buyer can act on

Working with CRM, product analytics, and pipeline data to measure funnel conversion, win rate, and deal size

Customer and market/competitor research, including win/loss interviews and call analysis

Systems thinking: connecting people, processes, data, and tools to identify opportunities and build repeatable solutions across the go-to-market motion

You lead without a formal title. You align sales, product, RevOps, and finance on goals, trade-offs, and what success looks like, then keep things moving.

You advocate for creators in decisions, and you protect the self-serve experience instead of pushing every account toward sales.

You act with urgency and stay focused on hitting OKRs and owning outcomes.

You work in public. You share findings, drafts, and the reasoning behind decisions so others can build on them.

You connect data to decisions, and you help others get sharper about using it.

You raise the bar on quality and iterate toward it, and you shape how your team uses AI, from call analysis to keeping battlecards current.

Experience in only self-serve or only enterprise sales. This role lives in the space between them, and you'll need to understand both.

Needing extensive approval before acting. You'll be trusted to make calls on speed versus quality and to explain your reasoning.

Discomfort with direct, candid feedback, especially from sellers on what's working in live deals.

#7448. Mercury: Engineering Manager - Bank Accounts

Roman aqueducts quietly carried water across cities and made daily life possible. Their value was not in the structures themselves, but in everything they enabled.

The Bank Accounts team plays a similar role at Mercury. We build the foundation for depository accounts*: managing money held in Mercury accounts and helping customers move money between accounts they own.

The work ahead is especially exciting: as Mercury is opening its own bank, this team will build a major part of the foundation needed to manage customer accounts at that scale. The systems and capabilities we build will support how customers open, use, manage, and move money between their accounts.

Your job as an engineering manager at Mercury is to make our banking* core run so smoothly that people barely notice it’s there.

We encourage you to try our demo site if you’re interested in applying for the role.

*Mercury is a fintech company, not an FDIC-insured bank. Banking services provided through Choice Financial Group and Column N.A., Members FDIC.

#7430. Lawsuit alleges chronic underfunding of University of Maryland Eastern Shore

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A group of students, faculty and alumni said the historically Black institution has been discriminated against and shortchanged $6 billion since its founding.

The plaintiffs are two UMES students and a corporate entity created last year called ForUMES, which, according to the complaint, is made up of university faculty, students, alumni and prospects and is aimed at bolstering the “educational, research, public service, and charitable objectives for UMES.”

The plaintiffs described UMES as “the woefully underfunded stepchild of the University System of Maryland.”

The complaint traces the funding disparity back to 1890, when Maryland wanted to take advantage of the federal Second Morrill Act.

That law sought to bolster the country’s land-grant universities with additional annual funding. But the money wasn’t available for states whose public land-grant institutions had racially discriminatory admissions unless they had a comparable college “of like character” for Black students, per the statute.

According to the lawsuit, Henry Alvord — who was then the president of University of Maryland, College Park, the state’s first land-grant institution — was “in a dilemma.”

The university “was in desperate need of additional funding but the idea of permitting Black students to attend [UMD] was unfathomable because his and many others’ racist beliefs would never allow [UMD] to be integrated,” the lawsuit states.

Alvord eventually found a solution to his dilemma in the Princess Anne Academy, a “small, remote and run-down preparatory school on the southern point of the eastern shore” that could — on paper — become a land-grant college for Black students, operated by Morgan College, which later became the HBCU Morgan State University.

However, the “lion’s share” of the new funding under the Second Morrill Act went to UMD, which did not admit Black students until a 1950 lawsuit.

“Black students would be sent to far-off Princess Anne to receive junior and senior high school-level classes on Princess Anne Academy’s dilapidated campus instead of a school of ‘like character’ to [UMD]’s state-of-the-art college,” plaintiffs said in the complaint.

According to the lawsuit, the underfunding and neglect of the academy — which later became UMES — continued throughout the 20th century and amid regular federal scrutiny of segregation in Maryland’s higher educational system and disparately low funding for UMES.

In 2023, then-President Joe Biden’s secretaries of education and agriculture wrote to Maryland Gov. Wes Moore — who is named as a defendant in the new lawsuit along with other top officials — to address the historical funding disparities at UMES as part of a broader effort to encourage more funding at historically Black land-grant colleges across the country.

“The longstanding and ongoing underinvestment in University of Maryland Eastern Shore disadvantages the students, faculty, and community that the institution serves,” the Biden officials wrote. “Furthermore, it may contribute to a lack of economic activity that would ultimately benefit Maryland.”

They calculated that UMES had been underfunded to the tune of $321.2 million just over the prior 30 years before the letter.

“These funds could have supported infrastructure and student services and would have better positioned the university to compete for research grants,” they said, while also acknowledging that “your state budget has taken steps to address this historic underinvestment.”

Since 2023, UMES has received around $9 million a year in additional state funding through a landmark Maryland law passed in 2021 aimed at expanding support for the state’s HBCUs and settling a lawsuit brought by the institutions’ supporters.

Enrollment has also been on the increase at the university. In fall 2024, UMES had 3,177 students, up 10.1% from five years prior, according to federal data.

The lawsuit’s plaintiffs don’t say how they calculated the $6 billion in damages they are seeking for UMES. In addition to monetary reparation for the years of underfunding, they asked the judge to require state officials to allocate land-grant funding equitably between UMD and UMES.

#7425. Head of Research at CoinMarketCap

CoinMarketCap is hiring a Head of Research to turn CMC data into research that institutions, media and partners trust and pay for. This is a senior, hands-on role reporting to the CMO.

CMC is the most referenced price and market data source in crypto. Our research already reaches millions each month through reports like our Monthly Exchange Report, Asset Watch and Market Pulse, and is cited by Bloomberg, WSJ, Forbes and Fortune. The next step is different. We are building CMC into a data platform for crypto, tokenized assets and broader markets, and research is how we prove the quality of that data to the people who buy it.

You will own our research agenda end to end: what we publish, how rigorous it is, and how it supports our enterprise data business. You will also be the senior voice on sensitive market analysis for CMC and its partners.

Set and run the research roadmap, from recurring flagships (monthly exchange report, weekly market and asset coverage) to deep dives on exchanges, tokenized assets, RWAs and market structure.

Build institutional grade research that showcases CMC data to banks, funds, data buyers and enterprise API clients, working closely with our API and BD teams on what buyers need.

Own methodology and data integrity: define how we measure volume, liquidity, flows and reserves, document it, and make sure every published number holds up to scrutiny.

Lead research on sensitive or fast moving market events, balancing speed, neutrality and accuracy, and handle review requests from partners with sound judgement.

Be a credible public face for CMC research with tier one media, on panels and in interviews.

Manage and develop a small research team, currently one research analyst, and use AI and automation to scale output without lowering quality.

Partner with marketing, editorial and product to turn research into formats people actually read and share, from long form reports to charts and short social posts.

7+ years in research or data analysis, with at least 3 in crypto or digital asset markets.

A track record of published, data heavy research that institutions or serious media rely on.

Deep understanding of market structure: exchanges, spot and derivatives, liquidity, onchain flows and proof of reserves.

Strong quantitative skills and hands on data work (SQL or Python, APIs, large datasets).

Excellent written English and the judgement to present sensitive findings neutrally and accurately.

Experience leading or mentoring analysts.

Background at a crypto data provider, an institutional research desk or a TradFi data business.

Understanding of how data is packaged and sold to institutions (licensing, enterprise feeds, research subscriptions).

Knowledge of tokenized assets, RWAs or traditional market data.

Existing relationships with tier one financial and crypto media.

Comfort using AI tools to automate data pulls and drafts.

Mandarin, given our close work with Chinese speaking partners and media.
